can be written as

\displaystyle\langle\varphi(\mathbf{k}_{1})\varphi(\mathbf{k}_{2})\gamma^{(\pm 2)}(\mathbf{k}_{3})\rangle^{\prime}=\mathcal{K}^{(\pm 2)}(\theta_{13},\theta_{23})\mathcal{J}^{(\pm 2)}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}),

(248)

where
\mathcal{K}^{(\pm 2)}(\theta_{13},\theta_{23})
is the kinematic factor arising from contracting the polarization tensor
e_{ij,\mathbf{k}_{3}}^{(\pm 2)}
with the external momenta:

\mathcal{K}^{(\pm 2)}(\theta_{13},\theta_{23})=e_{ij,\mathbf{k}_{3}}^{(\pm 2)}\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0muk\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1i}\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0muk\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2j}=-\sin\theta_{13}\sin\theta_{23},

(249)

with
\theta_{i3}

(i=1,2)
being the angles between
\mathbf{k}_{i}
and
\mathbf{k}_{3}
. So the kinematic factor depends only on the angles, and turns out to be independent of
h=\pm 2
. On the other hand, the factor
\mathcal{J}^{(\pm 2)}_{\mathsf{ab}}
characterizes the shape dependence, and is our main focus. Using the diagrammatic rule, it can be written as the following SK integral:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{(\pm 2)}_{\mathsf{ab}}=

\displaystyle-\mathsf{ab}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}k_{1}k_{2}\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{1}\displaystyle\frac{\,{{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{2}}\,}{(-\tau_{2})}G_{\mathsf{a}}(k_{1},\tau_{1})G_{\mathsf{a}}(k_{2},\tau_{1})\partial_{\tau_{2}}T_{\mathsf{b}}(k_{3},\tau_{2})D_{\mathsf{ab}}^{(\pm 2)}(k_{3};\tau_{1},\tau_{2}).

(250)

Here
G_{\mathsf{a}}(k,\tau)
is the bulk-to-boundary propagator of the inflaton fluctuation, given in (
39
), and
T_{\mathsf{a}}(k,\tau)
is the bulk-to-boundary propagator of the massless tensor mode, given by

T_{\mathsf{a}}(k,\tau)=\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{4k^{3}}(1-\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}k\tau)e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}k\tau}.

(251)

Substituting the various propagators into above expression, and noting that the transverse spin-2 propagator (
246
) is related to the transverse spin-1 propagator (
34
) via

D_{>}^{(\pm 2)}(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{2\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}D_{>}^{(\pm)}(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2}),

(252)

it is straightforward to rewrite the above 3-point function in terms of the vector seed integral (
43
) as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{(\pm 2)}_{\mathsf{ab}}=-\displaystyle\frac{\,{\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}}\,}{32(k_{1}k_{2}k_{3})^{2}}\Big(1+r\partial_{r}+\displaystyle\frac{\,{k_{1}k_{2}}\,}{k_{12}^{2}}(r^{2}\partial_{r}^{2}+2r\partial_{r})\Big){\mathcal{I}}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{(\pm)-1,-1}(r,1^{-}),

(253)

where we have defined
r\equiv k_{3}/k_{12}
.

Now we can make use of our folded limit results of the transverse vector seed integral derived in Sec.
5
. Insert (
5.2
) and (
209
) into
(
253
),
6
6

6

A gentle reminder that (
5.2
) and (
209
) are written as functions of
u
. We should rewrite all
u=2r/(1+r)
when inserting them into (
253
).
and also recall that
{\mathcal{I}}^{(h)-1,-1}_{\text{BG},>}=\mathcal{V}_{+}+\text{c.c.}
, we will obtain the signal piece and the background piece of the 3-point function
\mathcal{J}^{(\pm)}
, respectively.
The final result is relatively long, thus we will not write it down explicitly. Instead, we plot the results for near equilateral configurations in Fig.
9
and for squeezed configurations in Fig.
10
. From Fig.
9
, we see that the bispectrum is approximately in equilateral shape, and the shape has weak dependence on the chemical potential. On the contrary, we see from Fig.
10
that the chemical potential has a huge impact on the oscillatory signal: The whole result is dominated by the background when
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu=0
and dominated by the signal when
|\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu|
increases to
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
.

Figure 9:
The mixed bispectrum
(k_{1}k_{2}k_{3})^{2}\sum_{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}}\mathcal{J}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{(\pm 2)}
mediated by the helicity
h=\pm 2
component of a massive spin-2 field with mass parameter
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
and chemical potential
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
.

Figure 10:
The mixed bispectrum
\mathcal{S}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\equiv(k_{1}k_{2}k_{3})^{2}\sum_{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}}\mathcal{J}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{(\pm 2)}
mediated by the helicity
h=\pm 2
component of a massive spin-2 field with mass parameter
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
and chemical potential
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
. In this figure we choose isosceles and squeezed configurations with
k_{1}=k_{2}>k_{3}
.

Scalar bispectrum.

Finally, for completeness, we also write down an explicit expression for the scalar 3-point function mediated by a massive scalar as shown in Fig.
3
. This 3-point function has been written in terms of the scalar seed integral in (
58
), where we need to take the folded limit
r_{2}\rightarrow 1
.
In principle we can use our results for general scalar seed integrals, and set
r_{2}\rightarrow 1^{-}
to obtain the correct bispectrum. However, the background will be expressed in the form of a series,
and the convergence speed becomes slower when
r_{1}\rightarrow 1^{-}
.
On the other hand, in Sec.
5
we have derived a closed form for the background piece in the folded limit, so it is more convenient to use the bootstrap results when we analyze the bispectrum.
We need only one more step to take
\mathcal{I}^{-2,-2}_{>}
obtained in Sec.
5
to
\mathcal{I}_{>}^{0,-2}
as required by (
58
). This can be conveniently done by acting appropriate differential operators:

{\mathcal{I}}^{0,-2}_{>}(r_{1},r_{2})=-k_{s}^{2}\partial_{k_{12}}^{2}{\mathcal{I}}^{-2,-2}_{>}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{k_{s}}\,}{k_{12}},\displaystyle\frac{\,{k_{s}}\,}{k_{34}}\Big)=-r_{1}^{2}(r_{1}^{2}\partial_{r_{1}}^{2}+2r_{1}\partial_{r_{1}}){\mathcal{I}}^{-2,-2}_{>}(r_{1},r_{2}).

(254)

Therefore, when we go to the folded limit
r_{2}\rightarrow 1^{-}
, we obtain:

{\mathcal{I}}^{0,-2}_{>}(r_{1},1^{-})=-r_{1}^{2}(r_{1}^{2}\partial_{r_{1}}^{2}+2r_{1}\partial_{r_{1}}){\mathcal{I}}^{-2,-2}_{>}(r_{1},1^{-}).

(255)

Now we insert (
179
) and (
5.1
), we obtain the bispectrum:

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\phi}=

\displaystyle-\displaystyle\frac{\,{\lambda\lambda_{2}}\,}{8k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}^{4}}\pi\text{sech}(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\bigg\{\displaystyle\frac{\,{1+\mathrm{i}\sinh\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\,}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r}\,}{2}\Big)^{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\mathbf{F}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{0}(r)+\displaystyle\frac{\,{1-\mathrm{i}\sinh\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\,}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r}\,}{2}\Big)^{-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\mathbf{F}_{-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{0}(r)

\displaystyle+\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{2r}\,}{1+r}\Big)^{3}{}_{3}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}\left[\begin{matrix}1,1,1\\
\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{matrix}\middle|\,\displaystyle\frac{\,{2r}\,}{1+r}\right]+\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{2r}\,}{1+r}\Big)^{4}{}_{3}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}\left[\begin{matrix}2,2,2\\
\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{matrix}\middle|\,\displaystyle\frac{\,{2r}\,}{1+r}\right]

\displaystyle+2\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{2r}\,}{1+r}\Big)^{5}{}_{3}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}\left[\begin{matrix}3,3,3\\
\mbox{$\frac{\,{7}\,}{2}$}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,\mbox{$\frac{\,{7}\,}{2}$}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{matrix}\middle|\,\displaystyle\frac{\,{2r}\,}{1+r}\right]\bigg\},

(256)

where
\mathbf{F}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{p}(r)
is defined in (
78
), and
r\equiv k_{3}/k_{12}
.

7  Phenomenology

A major motivation for the study of inflation correlators is their potential observability in future observations of primordial non-Gaussianities. In particular, the presence of the helicity-dependent chemical potential opens up the possibility of scale invariant oscillatory signals with large amplitudes and large oscillation frequency at the same time, unlike most of the non-Gaussian shapes considered before. More importantly, a measurement of these oscillatory shapes would provide invaluable information about heavy particles and their interactions at the inflation scale, which is the main idea of CC physics.

The CC phenomenology of the helical inflation correlators has been studied in previous works in the context of 1-loop SM and BSM processes
[
61
,
75
,
70
,
71
,
79
]
, CP-violation
[
69
]
, higher spins
[
89
]
, the signal phase
[
35
]
, etc. The exact and analytical results we obtained in this work enables us to fast and precisely determine the shapes and sizes of the signal and the background as functions various model parameters, which would be useful for future phenomenological studies. Below, we shall discuss implications of our results for the study of CC signals. Since there have been quite a few works studying the phenomenological aspects of the chemical potential, we shall not be comprehensive in this section, but will mostly focus on the aspects most directly related to the results obtained in previous sections.

7.1
Cosmological collider signals in the squeezed limit

The so-called CC signals consist of oscillatory shapes in the logarithms of various momentum ratios in certain soft configurations of the inflation correlators. In the case of 4-point function, the most relevant signals come from the squeezed limit
r_{1,2}\rightarrow 0
, or equivalently,
u_{1,2}\rightarrow 0
. Therefore, let us first look at the signal integrals, including the local signal (
130
) and (
131
) for the transverse polarizations, and the corresponding signal integrals (
136
) and (
137
) for the longitudinal polarization.

Signals from transverse polarizations.

The transverse signals are of most interest, due to their exponential sensitivity to the chemical potential. To see this point, let us take the squeezed limit
u_{1,2}\rightarrow 0
of (
130
) and (
131
). For simplicity, we shall only consider the case of
p_{1}=p_{2}=0
.
Furthermore, notice that
\mathcal{G}^{(h)00}_{\text{L}/\text{NL}}(u_{1},u_{2})\propto(u_{1}u_{2})^{3/2}
in the squeezed limit. So, we remove the factor
(u_{1}u_{2})^{3/2}
by defining:

\mathcal{G}^{(h)00}_{\text{L}}(u_{1},u_{2})=(u_{1}u_{2})^{3/2}\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{(h)00}_{\text{L}}(u_{1},u_{2}),\qquad\mathcal{G}^{(h)00}_{\text{NL}}(u_{1},u_{2})=(u_{1}u_{2})^{3/2}\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{(h)00}_{\text{NL}}(u_{1},u_{2}).

(257)

We note that the factor
(u_{1}u_{2})^{3/2}
goes as
k_{s}^{3}
in the squeezed limit
k_{s}\rightarrow 0
, which is canceled by the factor
1/k_{s}^{3}
in the final result for the correlator such as
\mathcal{T}_{2}
in (
46
). So the signals do not decay away in the squeezed limit, where their sizes are controlled by the hatted quantities
\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{(h)00}_{\text{L}}
and
\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{(h)00}_{\text{NL}}
.
Then the squeezed limit can be expressed as

\displaystyle\lim_{u_{1},u_{2}\rightarrow 0}\mathcal{I}_{\text{L},>}^{(h)00}(u_{1},u_{2})=

\displaystyle~(u_{1}u_{2})^{3/2}\Big|\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{(h)00}_{\text{L}}(0,0)\Big|\cos\Big[\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\log\displaystyle\frac{\,{u_{1}}\,}{u_{2}}+\text{Arg}\,\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{(h)00}_{\text{L}}(0,0)\Big],

(258)

\displaystyle\lim_{u_{1},u_{2}\rightarrow 0}\mathcal{I}_{\text{NL},>}^{(h)00}(u_{1},u_{2})=

\displaystyle~(u_{1}u_{2})^{3/2}\Big|\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{(h)00}_{\text{NL}}(0,0)\Big|\cos\Big[\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\log(u_{1}u_{2})+\text{Arg}\,\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{(h)00}_{\text{NL}}(0,0)\Big].

(259)

Therefore, the local and nonlocal signals manifest as oscillations in
\log(u_{1}/u_{2})
and
\log(u_{1}u_{2})
, respectively, with the frequency given by the mass parameter
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
. Their sizes in the squeezed limit are respectively controlled by the coefficients
|\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{(h)00}_{\text{L}}(0,0)|
and
|\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{(h)00}_{\text{NL}}(0,0)|
, while the complex phases
\text{Arg}\,\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{(h)00}_{\text{L}}(0,0)
and
\text{Arg}\,\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{(h)00}_{\text{NL}}(0,0)
control the phases of the signal. The signal phase of this process has been more carefully studied in
[
35
]
. Here we shall only focus on the signal size.

From the explicit expressions (
4.3
) and (
4.3
), together with the definition (
257
), we can easily read the squeezed limits
u_{1,2}\rightarrow 0
of the functions
\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\text{L}}^{(h)p_{1}p_{2}}(u_{1},u_{2})
and
\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\text{NL}}^{(h)p_{1}p_{2}}(u_{1},u_{2})
. The results are:

\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\text{L}}^{(h)00}(0,0)=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{\pi(4\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}+1)}\,}{32\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu(e^{4\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}-1)}(e^{2\pi(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu-h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}+e^{2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}+\mathrm{i}e^{\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}-\mathrm{i}e^{3\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}),

(260)

\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\text{NL}}^{(h)00}(0,0)=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\pi^{2}e^{\pi(3\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu-h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}}\,}{2(e^{2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}-1)^{2}(e^{\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}-\mathrm{i})^{2}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{\Gamma^{2}(\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\,}{\Gamma^{2}(1+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\Gamma(\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}+h\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\Gamma(\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}-h\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}.

(261)

In particular, we can find the absolute values of these functions, as

\displaystyle\Big|\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\text{L}}^{(h)00}(0,0)\Big|=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{\pi(4\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}+1)}\,}{32\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu(e^{4\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}-1)}\sqrt{e^{6\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}+e^{4\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(e^{-4\pi h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}+2e^{-2\pi h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}-1)+e^{2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}},

(262)

\displaystyle\Big|\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\text{NL}}^{(h)00}(0,0)\Big|=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{\pi e^{-\pi h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(4\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}+1)}\,}{64\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\text{csch}(2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\sqrt{2\cosh 2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+2\cosh 2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}.

(263)

Thus we see that the signal sizes are exponentially sensitive to both the mass parameter
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
and the chemical potential
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
. The dependence on the chemical potential is of particular interest. We can see that, depending on the relative sizes of
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
and
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
, different terms in (
262
) and (
263
) dominate the result. It is straightforward to get the following approximated expressions, which make clear the exponential dependences on
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
and
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
for different intervals of parameter space:

\displaystyle\Big|\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\text{L}}^{(h)00}(0,0)\Big|\simeq

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{\pi(4\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}+1)}\,}{32\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\times\begin{cases}e^{-2\pi(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)},&(h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu<-\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\\
e^{-\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}.&(h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\geq-\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\end{cases}

(264)

\displaystyle\Big|\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\text{NL}}^{(h)00}(0,0)\Big|\simeq

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{\pi(4\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}+1)}\,}{32\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\times\begin{cases}e^{-2\pi(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)},&(h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu<-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\\
e^{-\pi(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)},&(-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\leq h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu<\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\\
e^{-2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}.&(h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\geq\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\end{cases}

(265)

In Fig.
11
, we plot the signal sizes (
262
) and (
263
) for fixed mass parameter
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu=3
as functions of helicity-weighted chemical potential
h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
. The broken-exponential behavior is evident from the figure. As expected, the presence of a chemical potential will exponentially enhance the signal with
h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu<0
and suppress the signal with
h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu>0
. On top of this general feature, there appears some finer structures as well. For instance, the degree of exponential enhancement is different for nonlocal signal with
h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu<0
for
|\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu|>\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
and
|\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu|<\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
. Also, the exponential suppressions for the nonlocal signal is saturated at
|\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu|=\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
. On the other hand, the local signal never receives exponential suppression for
h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu>0
, but receives exponential enhancement for
h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu<-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu/2
.

In particle model buildings, it often happens that the particles of both helicities
h=\pm 1
are present in the spectrum, and therefore the overall signal size is the sum of the contributions from both helicities. As a result, we can see from Fig.
11
that the nonlocal signal always dominates over the local signal when
|\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu|<\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
, but is of comparable size with the local signal when
|\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu|\geq\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
.
7
7

7

In
[
41
]
it was mentioned that the local signal is insensitive to the chemical potential. It seems to us that this conclusion was drawn by looking at the small chemical potential limit
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\ll\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
. Our result shown in Fig.
11
confirms this observation around
h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu=0
but shows otherwise for
|\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu|>\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu/2
.

Figure 11:
The sizes of local and nonlocal signals from the helical 4-point function mediated by a massive spin-1 state with helicity
h=\pm 1
, mass parameter
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu=3
, and chemical potential
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
.

Signals from the longitudinal polarization.

The signals from the longitudinal polarization is simpler, and is totally independent of the chemical potential. To find the signal sizes, we again expand the signal integrals (
136
) and (
137
) in the squeezed limit
r_{1,2}\rightarrow 0
, and take
p_{1}=p_{2}=0
for simplicity. Similar to the transverse case, the signal is governed by the functions
\mathcal{G}_{\text{L}}^{(L)00}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\text{NL}}^{(L)00}
at
r_{1}=r_{2}=0
.
Similarly, to remove the dilation factor in
\mathcal{G}^{(L)00}_{\text{L}}
and
\mathcal{G}^{(L)00}_{\text{NL}}
, we define:

\mathcal{G}^{(L)00}_{\text{L}}(r_{1},r_{2})=(r_{1}r_{2})^{3/2}\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{(L)00}_{\text{L}}(r_{1},r_{2}),\qquad\mathcal{G}^{(L)00}_{\text{NL}}(r_{1},r_{2})=(r_{1}r_{2})^{3/2}\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{(L)00}_{\text{NL}}(r_{1},r_{2}).

(266)

From the definition above and the expressions in (
138
) and (
139
), we get

\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\text{L}}^{(L)00}(0,0)=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{{\pi}(1+4\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2})}\,}{4\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\big(1-\mathrm{i}\sinh\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\big)\,\text{csch}\,2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,

(267)

\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\text{NL}}^{(L)00}(0,0)=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{1+4\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}}\,}{8{\pi}}\Gamma^{2}(-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\Gamma^{2}\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\big)\big(1-\mathrm{i}\sinh\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\big).

(268)

In particular, the signal sizes are given by the absolute values of these functions, which turn out to be the same for both the local and the nonlocal signals:

\displaystyle\Big|\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\text{L}}^{(L)00}(0,0)\Big|=\Big|\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\text{NL}}^{(L)00}(0,0)\Big|=\displaystyle\frac{\,{{\pi}(1+4\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2})}\,}{8\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\,\text{csch}\,\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu.

(269)

As expected, the signal size is exponentially damped
\propto e^{-\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
for large mass parameter
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
.

We note that in both the transverse and longitudinal signal expressions, there appears a divergence as the mass parameter
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow 0
, which corresponds to the borderline between the principal and complementary series. While some enhancement of the signal is expected for
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\ll 1
, the divergence itself must be unphysical, since the perturbative expansion itself breaks down if we come too close to
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu=0
. Therefore we expect that the divergence around this point should be removed by resumming higher order diagrams. We leave this issue for future studies.

7.2
Background

We next look at the background piece, given by (
4.3
) for the transverse polarizations and by (
4.3
) for the longitudinal polarization. Again we will only consider the case
p_{1}=p_{2}=0
. The background piece is not as easy to compute or even to estimate compared with the signal part. The late-time expansion of the massive propagator that is often used to estimate the signal size is inapplicable for background estimation. On the other hand, we know that the background piece corresponds to the local EFT contributions after we integrate out the intermediate heavy particle. Therefore, in the case of a dS covariant correlator mediated by a normal particle of mass
m
(without dS boost-breaking chemical potentials), we know that the background contribution should be proportional to
1/m^{2}
in the large
m
limit. However, it is less clear if the chemical potential would affect this estimate, especially when the chemical potential becomes large.

With the full analytical result at hand, we can give this question a definite answer. In short, the background piece is dependent on the chemical potential, but in a rather weak way: There is no exponential enhancement of the background even when the chemical potential is large. Therefore, with our exact results, we confirm a previous expectation that the CC signal could be parametrically larger than the background for scenarios with chemical potential enhancement.

Since the longitudinal correlator is in any case independent of the chemical potential, here we will only consider the transverse background piece (
4.3
).

Figure 12:
The transverse component of the background integral
\mathcal{I}_{\text{BG},>}^{(h)00}
in (
4.3
) at
u_{1}=u_{2}=0.5
. LEFT:
\mathcal{I}_{\text{BG},>}^{(h)00}
as functions of helicity-weighted chemical potential
h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
for several choices of mass parameters
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
. RIGHT:
\mathcal{I}_{\text{BG},>}^{(h)00}
as functions of
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
for several values of
h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
.

A first hint that the background piece is insensitive to the chemical potential appears when we look at the squeezed limit
r_{1}<r_{2}\ll 1
, where only the
n_{1}=n_{2}=0
term contribute to the series in (
4.3
):

\displaystyle\lim_{r_{1}\ll 1}\mathcal{I}_{\text{BG},>}^{(h)00}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}u_{1}^{3}}\,}{8\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\mathcal{F}\bigg[\begin{matrix}\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,3\\
\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{matrix}\bigg|-\displaystyle\frac{\,{u_{1}}\,}{u_{2}}\bigg]+\text{c.c.}.

(270)

In particular, when we take a “hierarchical” squeezed limit
r_{1}\ll r_{2}
(which implies
r_{1}\ll 1
since
r_{2}<1
but
r_{2}
is not required small), the above expression gets further simplified to

\displaystyle\lim_{r_{1}\ll r_{2}}\mathcal{I}_{\text{BG},>}^{(h)00}=-\displaystyle\frac{\,{u_{1}^{3}}\,}{9+4\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}}.

(271)

Therefore we see that the background piece is completely independent of the chemical potential in the squeezed limit. If the background piece were exponentially sensitive to chemical potential for nonsqueezed configurations, and in particular, were exponentially enhanced when the chemical potential is large, then it would follow that the background piece has an exponential dependence on the various momentum ratios, a behavior that is unlikely to be generated by local EFT operators. Therefore we expect that the chemical potential dependence in the background piece, if present at all, should be a weak one.

Figure 13:
The transverse vector seed integrals for
r_{1}=r_{2}\equiv r
(namely
k_{12}=k_{34}
) as functions of
r
. The magenta curve corresponds to the signal integral, namely
\mathcal{I}_{\text{L},>}^{(h)00}+\mathcal{I}_{\text{NL},>}^{(h)00}
in (
130
) and (
131
), the blue curve shows the background integral
\mathcal{I}_{\text{BG},>}^{(h)00}
in (
4.3
), and the black curves show the sum of the signal and the background. In both panels we choose
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu=2
, and we choose
h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu=0
for the left panel and
h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu=-1
for the right panel.

This expectation is confirmed by explicitly plotting the chemical potential dependence for nonsqueezed configurations. In Fig.
12
, we show the dependence of the background integral (
4.3
) of the transverse component on the helicity-weighted chemical potential
h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
and also on the mass parameter
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
. In this figure we choose a nonsqueezed configuration
u_{1}=u_{2}=0.5
. It is clear from the figure that the chemical potential affects the size of the background piece (for a nonsqueezed configuration) only in a rather mild way (i.e., no order of magnitude changes), even when the chemical potential
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
becomes greater than the mass parameter
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
.

From the above discussion of the signal strength and the background size, we find a particularly interesting feature of the CC signals from chemical potential mechanism: When we increase the chemical potential from zero to a value comparable to or greater than the mass parameter, the size of the oscillatory signal will be exponentially enhanced, while the smooth background piece is largely insensitive to the change of the chemical potential. Therefore, the signal can be parametrically larger than the background. We illustrate this feature in Fig.
13
in which we show both the signal and the background for configurations with
r_{1}=r_{2}
. For the parameter
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu=2
used in the figure, it is evident that the background dominates the total result when
h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu=0
and the signal already dominates over the total result when we choose
h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu=-1
. Had we chosen
|h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu|\geq 2
, the background would be negligibly small compared to the signal and the difference between the signal and the total result would be invisible.

Figure 14:
The transverse vector seed integral (
126
) as functions of the two momentum ratios
r_{1}=k_{s}/k_{12}
and
r_{2}=k_{s}/k_{34}
. Roughly, the oscillations in the direction from lower-left to upper-right correspond to the nonlocal signal, while the oscillations in the direction from upper-left to lower-right correspond to the local signal.

The analytical expressions we obtained in this work enable us to easily generate plots for the shapes of the 4-point correlators. We show several examples in Fig.
14
. In all these examples, we have chosen the chemical potential
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
to be equal or close to the mass parameter
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
. In such cases, the signal always dominates over the background, as is manifested in the figure. By choosing two different mass parameters
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu=3
(upper panels) and
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu=10
(lower panels), we show how the frequency of the signal is changing with the mass. By choosing two different chemical potentials
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
for each mass parameter (left and right panels), we show the sensitive dependence of the signals on
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
.

8  Conclusions and Outlooks

The helical inflation correlators are a special class of inflation correlators mediated by massive spinning particles and boosted by helicity-dependent chemical potentials. They arise naturally from a wide range of particle models for cosmological collider physics with distinct phenomenology and promising observation prospects. On the other hand, these correlators break the dS boosts and the space parity, which renders their analytical calculation more difficult than dS covariant correlators.

In this work, we have attacked the problem of analytical computation of helical inflation correlators with two independent methods. In the first method, we have used the partial Mellin-Barnes representation to resolve the complicated intermediate massive propagators into products of simple power functions. The originally difficult SK time integrals were thus transformed into more tractable contour integrals on the complex planes of Mellin variables. In the second method, we have derived the equations, namely the bootstrap equations, satisfied by ordinary and helical inflation correlators, and showed that these correlators can be bootstrapped by solving the bootstrap equations with appropriate boundary conditions. In this process, we have reduced much of the computation of the correlators to that of a scalar seed integral and a vector seed integral. We further demonstrated how to use these building blocks to compute more general correlators, by working out a special example of mixed 3-point function with two scalar modes and one tensor mode. Finally, we have also discussed the phenomenology associated with the helical inflation correlators.

Our work represents one step forward in the application of the partial MB representation in the computation of cosmic correlators. There are much left to be done. One obvious extension is to include both the non-unit sound speed and the nonzero chemical potential, so that one can build up the most general boost-breaking correlators mediated by spinning particles. Also, one could try out the method with more wide range of correlators, with different topologies (tree diagrams and loop diagrams), different couplings. As can be seen, the partial MB representation has few requirements on the symmetry of the problem, and thus can be particularly suitable to deal with problems with less symmetries. One can also imagine to use this method to compute correlators in non-inflation background, as long as the relevant mode functions have known MB representations.

Also, as mentioned before, our result has explicitly verified a recently proposed tree-level cutting for CC signals for helical inflation correlators. From our calculation one can see that the partial MB representation can neatly separate terms with different analytical properties in the squeezed limit, which makes it a possible tool for mathematically proving the tree-level signal cutting rule. We leave this proof for a future work.

When bootstrapping the helical correlators, we have imposed the boundary conditions from the squeezed limit, which corresponds to taking the late-time expansion of the intermediate propagator in a bulk calculation. This is an opposite approach compared with existing bootstrap works in the literature, where the boundary conditions were normally imposed from the factorized limit and the folded limit. While we have checked in this work that all folded limits of our results are regular, we have not considered the unphysical limits such as the zero-total-energy limit or the zero-partial-energy limit (namely the factorized limit). It would be interesting to look into these limits more carefully for helical correlators.

In this work, we have only touched the spin-1 and spin-2 examples. It is possible to generalize the chemical potential to even higher spin fields, and also to half-integer spin fields. It would thus be interesting to understand more systematically the quantization and the dynamics of higher spin fields in the presence of such chemical potentials, and to use the existing analytical methods to compute the corresponding correlators with higher-spin or half-integer-spin mediations.

Finally, there is a particularly interesting parameter range where the oscillating signal could be much greater than the background. Therefore, current search for non-Gaussianities in the trispectrum could be insensitive to these shapes unless one includes those scale-invariant oscillations into the templates. Since we have provided the full results for the 4-point function mediated by massive spinning particles, including the signal and the background, it should be straightforward to fast generate template banks for these shapes and use them to search for potential signals in the trispectrum or to put more stringent constraints. We leave this more phenomenological study for a future work as well.

Appendix A
Useful Formulae

Useful functions.

Below we list some of the frequently used functions in this paper. First, we use the following shorthand notation for the products and factions of Euler
\Gamma
function:

\displaystyle\Gamma\left[z_{1},\cdots,z_{m}\right]\equiv

\displaystyle~\Gamma(z_{1})\cdots\Gamma(z_{m}),

(272)

\displaystyle\Gamma\left[\begin{matrix}z_{1},\cdots,z_{m}\\
w_{1},\cdots,w_{n}\end{matrix}\right]\equiv

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{\Gamma(z_{1})\cdots\Gamma(z_{m})}\,}{\Gamma(w_{1})\cdots\Gamma(w_{n})}.

(273)

We use the Pochhammer symbol
(z)_{n}
to simplify some expressions, which is defined as

\displaystyle(z)_{n}\equiv\Gamma\left[\begin{matrix}z+n\\
z\end{matrix}\right].

(274)

The (generalized) hypergeometric functions
{}_{p}\mathrm{F}_{q}
is formally defined via the following series.

\displaystyle{}_{p}\mathrm{F}_{q}\left[\begin{matrix}a_{1},\cdots,a_{p}\\
b_{1},\cdots,b_{q}\end{matrix}\middle|z\right]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(a_{1})_{n}\cdots(a_{p})_{n}}\,}{(b_{1})_{n}\cdots(b_{q})_{n}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{z^{n}}\,}{n!}.

(275)

In this work we will only encounter the case with
p=q+1
. In this case, the above series converges within the disk
|z|<1
. Outside this range, the function is defined via analytical continuation.

We employ the following
regularized
hypergeometric function when we want to highlight the analytical structure of some MB integrand:

{}_{p}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{q}\left[\begin{matrix}a_{1},\cdots,a_{p}\\
b_{1},\cdots,b_{q}\end{matrix}\middle|z\right]=\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{\Gamma[b_{1},\cdots,b_{q}]}\,{}_{p}\mathrm{F}_{q}\left[\begin{matrix}a_{1},\cdots,a_{p}\\
b_{1},\cdots,b_{q}\end{matrix}\middle|z\right],

(276)

which has the nice property that its principal branch is an entire function of all orders
a_{1},\cdots,a_{p}
and
b_{1},\cdots,b_{q}
.

We also use the following
dressed
hypergeometric functions to simplify some of expressions. We shall suppress the subscripts
p
and
q
without causing confusions.

{\mathcal{F}}\left[\begin{matrix}a_{1},\cdots,a_{p}\\
b_{1},\cdots,b_{q}\end{matrix}\middle|z\right]=\Gamma\left[\begin{matrix}a_{1},\cdots,a_{p}\\
b_{1},\cdots,b_{q}\end{matrix}\right]{}_{p}\mathrm{F}_{q}\left[\begin{matrix}a_{1},\cdots,a_{p}\\
b_{1},\cdots,b_{q}\end{matrix}\middle|z\right].

(277)

Useful integrals.

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}\tau\,e^{\pm\mathrm{i}k\tau}(-\tau)^{p-1}=e^{\mp\mathrm{i}p\pi/2}k^{-p}\Gamma(p),

(278)

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{2}\int_{\tau_{2}}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{1}\,e^{\pm(\mathrm{i}k_{12}\tau_{1}+\mathrm{i}k_{34}\tau_{2})}(-\tau_{1})^{p-1}(-\tau_{2})^{q-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~e^{\mp\mathrm{i}(p+q)\pi/2}k_{12}^{-p}k_{34}^{-q}\Gamma\Big[p,q\Big]-e^{\mp\mathrm{i}(p+q)\pi/2}k_{12}^{-p-q}\mathcal{F}\left[\begin{matrix}q,p+q\\
1+q\end{matrix}\middle|\,-\displaystyle\frac{\,{k_{34}}\,}{k_{12}}\right],

(279)

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\tau_{2}}{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{1}\,e^{\pm(\mathrm{i}k_{12}\tau_{1}+\mathrm{i}k_{34}\tau_{2})}(-\tau_{1})^{p-1}(-\tau_{2})^{q-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~e^{\mp\mathrm{i}(p+q)\pi/2}k_{12}^{-p-q}\mathcal{F}\left[\begin{matrix}q,p+q\\
1+q\end{matrix}\middle|\,-\displaystyle\frac{\,{k_{34}}\,}{k_{12}}\right].

(280)

Appendix B
General Trilinear Couplings

In this appendix we present the full expression for the 4-point correlator generated by the general coupling of the form given in (
37
). Specifically, we consider two such couplings,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{1}}
and
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{2}}
, with
\mathbb{P}_{i}=(J_{i},K_{i},M_{i},N_{i},R_{i})

(i=1,2)
. Then, following the diagrammatic rule, we can write down the expression corresponding to the
s
-channel exchange of a massive spin-1 field as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\text{2}}^{(h)}=

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathbb{P}_{1}}\lambda_{\mathbb{P}_{2}}(-\mathbf{k}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{2})^{M_{1}}(-\mathbf{k}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{4})^{M_{2}}(-k_{1}^{2})^{N_{1}}(-k_{2}^{2})^{R_{1}}(-k_{3}^{2})^{N_{2}}(-k_{4}^{2})^{R_{2}}(\mathbf{k}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{k}_{s}}^{(h)})(\mathbf{k}_{4}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{k}_{s}}^{(h)*})

\displaystyle~\times\sum_{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}=\pm}\mathsf{ab}\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{1}{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{2}\,(-\tau_{1})^{Q_{1}}(-\tau_{2})^{Q_{2}}\,\partial_{\tau_{1}}^{J_{1}}G_{\mathsf{a}}(k_{1},\tau_{1})\partial_{\tau_{1}}^{K_{1}}G_{\mathsf{a}}(k_{2},\tau_{1})

\displaystyle~\times\partial_{\tau_{2}}^{J_{2}}G_{\mathsf{b}}(k_{3},\tau_{2})\partial_{\tau_{2}}^{K_{2}}G_{\mathsf{b}}(k_{4},\tau_{2})D_{\mathsf{ab}}^{(h)}(k_{s};\tau_{1},\tau_{2}),

(281)

where
Q_{i}\equiv J_{i}+K_{i}+2(M_{i}+N_{i}+R_{i})-2
with
i=1,2
. Then, using the following equation for arbitrary number of temporal derivatives on the bulk-to-boundary propagator:

\partial_{\tau}^{J}\varphi(k,\tau)=\displaystyle\frac{\,{H}\,}{\sqrt{2k^{3}}}(-\mathrm{i}k)^{J}(1-J+\mathrm{i}k\tau)e^{-\mathrm{i}k\tau},

(282)

we get,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\text{2}}^{(h)}=

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathbb{P}_{1}}\lambda_{\mathbb{P}_{2}}(-1)^{M_{1}+M_{2}+N_{1}+N_{2}+R_{1}+R_{2}}k_{1}^{M_{1}+2N_{1}}k_{2}^{M_{1}+2R_{1}}k_{3}^{M_{2}+2N_{2}}k_{4}^{M_{2}+2R_{2}}\cos^{M_{1}}\theta_{12}\cos^{M_{2}}\theta_{34}

\displaystyle~\times(\mathbf{k}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{k}_{s}}^{(h)})(\mathbf{k}_{4}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{k}_{s}}^{(h)*})\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{16(k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4})^{3}}\sum_{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}=\pm}\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}(\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}k_{1})^{J_{1}}(\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}k_{2})^{K_{1}}(\mathrm{i}\mathsf{b}k_{3})^{J_{2}}(\mathrm{i}\mathsf{b}k_{4})^{K_{2}}

\displaystyle~\times\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{1}{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{2}\,(-\tau_{1})^{Q_{1}}(-\tau_{2})^{Q_{2}}(1-J_{1}-\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}k_{1}\tau_{1})(1-K_{1}-\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}k_{2}\tau_{1})

\displaystyle~\times(1-J_{2}-\mathrm{i}\mathsf{b}k_{3}\tau_{2})(1-K_{2}-\mathrm{i}\mathsf{b}k_{4}\tau_{2})e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}k_{12}\tau_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mathsf{b}k_{34}\tau_{2}}D_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{(h)}(k_{s};\tau_{1},\tau_{2}).

(283)

If we define:

v_{1}=k_{1}/k_{s},\quad v_{2}=k_{2}/k_{s},\quad v_{3}=k_{3}/k_{s},\quad v_{4}=k_{4}/k_{s},

(284)

then, in terms of these momentum ratios and the vector seed integral defined in (
43
), we can write the final answer to this process as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\text{2}}^{(h)}=

\displaystyle~\lambda_{\mathbb{P}_{1}}\lambda_{\mathbb{P}_{2}}(-1)^{M_{1}+M_{2}+N_{1}+N_{2}+R_{1}+R_{2}}v_{1}^{M_{1}+2N_{1}+J_{1}}v_{2}^{M_{1}+2R_{1}+K_{1}}v_{3}^{M_{2}+2N_{2}+J_{2}}v_{4}^{M_{2}+2R_{2}+K_{2}}

\displaystyle~\times\cos^{M_{1}}\theta_{12}\cos^{M_{2}}\theta_{34}(\mathbf{k}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{k}_{s}}^{(h)})(\mathbf{k}_{4}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{k}_{s}}^{(h)*})\displaystyle\frac{\,{-k_{s}}\,}{16(k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4})^{3}}\sum_{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}=\pm}(\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a})^{J_{1}+K_{1}}(\mathrm{i}\mathsf{b})^{J_{2}+K_{2}}

\displaystyle~\times\Big\{(1-J_{1})(1-K_{1})(1-J_{2})(1-K_{2})\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{(h)Q_{1},Q_{2}}+v_{1}v_{2}v_{3}v_{4}\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{(h)Q_{1}+2,Q_{2}+2}

\displaystyle~-\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}\big[(1-J_{1})v_{2}+(1-K_{1})v_{1}\big]\big[(1-J_{2})v_{4}+(1-K_{2})v_{3}\big]\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{(h)Q_{1}+1,Q_{2}+1}

\displaystyle~-\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}\big[(1-J_{1})v_{2}+(1-K_{1})v_{1}\big]\Big[(1-J_{2})(1-K_{2})\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{(h)Q_{1}+1,Q_{2}}-v_{3}v_{4}\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{(h)Q_{1}+1,Q_{2}+2}\Big]

\displaystyle~-\mathrm{i}\mathsf{b}\big[(1-J_{2})v_{4}+(1-K_{2})v_{3}\big]\Big[(1-J_{1})(1-K_{1})\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{(h)Q_{1},Q_{2}+1}-v_{1}v_{2}\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{(h)Q_{1}+2,Q_{2}+1}\Big]

\displaystyle~-v_{1}v_{2}(1-J_{2})(1-K_{2})\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{(h)Q_{1}+2,Q_{2}}-v_{3}v_{4}(1-J_{1})(1-K_{1})\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{(h)Q_{1},Q_{2}+2}\Big\}.

(285)

Appendix C
Fully Resolved MB Representation

In this appendix, we show that the vector seed integral (
43
) can also be computed using the fully resolved MB representation for the Whittaker function (
88
). The resulting expression would be functions of momentum ratios
r_{1}=k_{s}/k_{12}
and
r_{2}=k_{s}/k_{34}
instead of
u_{1,2}=2r_{1,2}/(1+r_{1,2})
. With the fully resolved representation, the propagators of massive spin-1 field can be written as

\displaystyle D_{\lessgtr}^{(h)}(k_{s};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{e^{-h\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}}\,}{2k_{s}}\text{W}_{\mp\mathrm{i}h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,\mp\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(\mp 2\mathrm{i}k_{s}\tau_{1})\text{W}_{\pm\mathrm{i}h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(\pm 2\mathrm{i}k_{s}\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~e^{-h\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\int_{-\mathrm{i}\infty}^{\mathrm{i}\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{{\mathrm{d}}s_{1}}\,}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{{\mathrm{d}}s_{2}}\,}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\,e^{\mp\mathrm{i}\pi(s_{1}-s_{2})/2}(2k_{s})^{-s_{12}}(-\tau_{1})^{-s_{1}+1/2}(-\tau_{2})^{-s_{2}+1/2}

\displaystyle\times\Gamma\Big[s_{1}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,s_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,s_{2}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,s_{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\Big]

\displaystyle\times{}_{2}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}s_{1}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,s_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
s_{1}\pm\mathrm{i}h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\end{matrix}\middle|\,\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\right]{}_{2}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}s_{2}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,s_{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
s_{2}\mp\mathrm{i}h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\end{matrix}\middle|\,\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\right],

(286)

Plugging the above representation into our definition for the vector seed integral (
43
), we can complete the time integral. It is easy to carry out the opposite-sign integrals:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{\pm\mp}^{(h)p_{1}p_{2}}=

\displaystyle~e^{-h\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}r_{1}^{3/2+p_{1}}r_{2}^{3/2+p_{2}}e^{\mp\mathrm{i}(p_{1}-p_{2})\pi/2}\int_{-\mathrm{i}\infty}^{\mathrm{i}\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{{\mathrm{d}}s_{1}}\,}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{{\mathrm{d}}s_{2}}\,}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\,(2r_{1})^{-s_{1}}(2r_{2})^{-s_{2}}

\displaystyle\times\Gamma\Big[p_{1}+\displaystyle\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}-s_{1},p_{2}+\displaystyle\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}-s_{2},s_{1}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,s_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,s_{2}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,s_{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\Big]

\displaystyle\times{}_{2}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}s_{1}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,s_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
s_{1}\pm\mathrm{i}h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\end{matrix}\middle|\,\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\right]{}_{2}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}s_{2}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,s_{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
s_{2}\mp\mathrm{i}h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\end{matrix}\middle|\,\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\right].

(287)

For the same-sign integrals, we still focus on the case that
k_{12}>k_{34}
. Similar to the scalar case and the vector case, the integral can be divided into a factorized part
{\mathcal{I}}^{(h)p_{1}p_{2}}_{\pm\pm,\text{F},>}
and a time-ordered part
{\mathcal{I}}^{(h)p_{1}p_{2}}_{\pm\pm,\text{TO},>}
, contributing to the signal and the background, respectively. Using the formulae given in App.
A
, we obtain:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{\pm\pm,\text{F},>}^{(h)p_{1}p_{2}}=

\displaystyle~e^{-h\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}r_{1}^{3/2+p_{1}}r_{2}^{3/2+p_{2}}e^{\mp\mathrm{i}(p_{1}+p_{2})\pi/2}\int_{-\mathrm{i}\infty}^{\mathrm{i}\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{{\mathrm{d}}s_{1}}\,}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{{\mathrm{d}}s_{2}}\,}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\,(\mp\mathrm{i}e^{\pm\mathrm{i}\pi s_{1}})(2r_{1})^{-s_{1}}(2r_{2})^{-s_{2}}

\displaystyle\times\Gamma\Big[p_{1}+\displaystyle\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}-s_{1},p_{2}+\displaystyle\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}-s_{2},s_{1}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,s_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,s_{2}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,s_{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\Big]

\displaystyle\times{}_{2}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}s_{1}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,s_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
s_{1}\mp\mathrm{i}h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\end{matrix}\middle|\,\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\right]{}_{2}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}s_{2}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,s_{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
s_{2}\pm\mathrm{i}h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\end{matrix}\middle|\,\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\right],

(288)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{\pm\pm,\text{TO},>}^{(h)p_{1}p_{2}}=

\displaystyle~e^{-h\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}r_{1}^{3+p_{1}+p_{2}}e^{\mp\mathrm{i}(p_{1}+p_{2})\pi/2}\int_{-\mathrm{i}\infty}^{\mathrm{i}\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{{\mathrm{d}}s_{1}}\,}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{{\mathrm{d}}s_{2}}\,}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\,

\displaystyle\times\bigg[(\pm\mathrm{i}e^{\pm\mathrm{i}\pi s_{1}}){}_{2}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}s_{1}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,s_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
s_{1}\mp\mathrm{i}h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\end{matrix}\middle|\,\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\right]{}_{2}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}s_{2}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,s_{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
s_{2}\pm\mathrm{i}h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\end{matrix}\middle|\,\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\right]

\displaystyle~~+(\mp\mathrm{i}e^{\pm\mathrm{i}\pi s_{2}}){}_{2}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}s_{1}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,s_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
s_{1}\pm\mathrm{i}h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\end{matrix}\middle|\,\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\right]{}_{2}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}s_{2}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,s_{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
s_{2}\mp\mathrm{i}h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\end{matrix}\middle|\,\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\right]\bigg]

\displaystyle\times(2r_{1})^{-s_{12}}{}_{2}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}p_{2}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}-s_{2},p_{1}+p_{2}+3-s_{12}\\
p_{2}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}-s_{2}\end{matrix}\middle|\,-\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{1}}\,}{r_{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\times\Gamma\Big[p_{2}+\displaystyle\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}-s_{2},p_{1}+p_{2}+3-s_{12},s_{1}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,s_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,s_{2}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,s_{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\Big].

(289)

Finally, we close the contour from left and pick up poles (
94
) and (
95
) (notice that the contribution of (
95
) vanishes for the time-ordered part), and obtain the (local and nonlocal) signals and the background piece:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{\text{L},>}^{(h)p_{1}p_{2}}=

\displaystyle~e^{-h\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}r_{1}^{3/2+p_{1}}r_{2}^{3/2+p_{2}}\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}=0}^{\infty}\bigg\{\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{n_{12}}}\,}{n_{1}!n_{2}!}\Big(e^{\mathrm{i}(p_{1}-p_{2})\pi/2}-(-1)^{n_{1}}\mathrm{i}e^{+\mathrm{i}(p_{1}+p_{2})\pi/2}e^{\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\Big)

\displaystyle\times(2r_{1})^{n_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(2r_{2})^{n_{2}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\times\Gamma\Big[n_{1}+p_{1}+\displaystyle\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,n_{2}+p_{2}+\displaystyle\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,-n_{1}-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,-n_{2}+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\Big]

\displaystyle\times{}_{2}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}-n_{1},-n_{1}-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
-n_{1}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu-\mathrm{i}h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\end{matrix}\middle|\,\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\right]{}_{2}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}-n_{2},-n_{2}+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
-n_{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+\mathrm{i}h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\end{matrix}\middle|\,\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\right]

\displaystyle+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\bigg\}+\text{c.c.},

(290)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{\text{NL}}^{(h)p_{1}p_{2}}=

\displaystyle~e^{-h\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}r_{1}^{3/2+p_{1}}r_{2}^{3/2+p_{2}}\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}=0}^{\infty}\bigg\{\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{n_{12}}}\,}{n_{1}!n_{2}!}\Big(e^{\mathrm{i}(p_{1}-p_{2})\pi/2}-(-1)^{n_{1}}\mathrm{i}e^{+\mathrm{i}(p_{1}+p_{2})\pi/2}e^{\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\Big)

\displaystyle\times(2r_{1})^{n_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(2r_{2})^{n_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\times\Gamma\Big[n_{1}+p_{1}+\displaystyle\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,n_{2}+p_{2}+\displaystyle\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,-n_{1}-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,-n_{2}-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\Big]

\displaystyle\times{}_{2}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}-n_{1},-n_{1}-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
-n_{1}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu-\mathrm{i}h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\end{matrix}\middle|\,\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\right]{}_{2}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}-n_{2},-n_{2}-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
-n_{2}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+\mathrm{i}h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\end{matrix}\middle|\,\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\right]

\displaystyle+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\bigg\}+\text{c.c.},

(291)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{\text{BG},>}^{(h)p_{1}p_{2}}=

\displaystyle~e^{-h\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}r_{1}^{3+p_{1}+p_{2}}\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}=0}^{\infty}\bigg\{\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{n_{12}}}\,}{n_{1}!n_{2}!}\times\mathrm{i}\Big[(-1)^{n_{1}}e^{-\mathrm{i}(p_{1}+p_{2})\pi/2}+(-1)^{n_{2}}e^{+\mathrm{i}(p_{1}+p_{2})\pi/2}\Big]e^{\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(2r_{1})^{n_{12}}

\displaystyle\times{}_{2}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}-n_{1},-n_{1}-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
-n_{1}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu-\mathrm{i}h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\end{matrix}\middle|\,\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\right]{}_{2}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}-n_{2},-n_{2}+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
-n_{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+\mathrm{i}h\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\end{matrix}\middle|\,\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\right]

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{F}\left[\begin{matrix}n_{2}+p_{2}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,n_{12}+p_{1}+p_{2}+3\\
n_{2}+p_{2}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{matrix}\middle|\,-\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{1}}\,}{r_{2}}\right]\times\Gamma\Big[-n_{1}-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,-n_{2}+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\Big]

\displaystyle+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\bigg\}+\text{c.c.}.

(292)

We have numerically checked that these are identical to our results in Sec.
4.3
.
